Space. 🌌

Now that is a subject with the decency to be appropriately enormous.

You mean space-space? As in the universe, stars, civilizations, black holes, the fact that the observable universe is roughly 93 billion light-years across despite being only 13.8 billion years old, and humanity has somehow decided its primary response should be arguing about parking?

Or are you arriving at the philosophical version of space, given where our conversation has been heading: being, emergence, intelligence, time, civilization, and what happens when minds become capable of reaching beyond the planet that produced them?

Because there is a particularly nasty and beautiful question sitting underneath all of that:

What happens to a civilization when its physical environment becomes larger than its inherited mythology?

Earth is one environment. Space is another. And almost every human institution, instinct, religion, economy, war doctrine, and political boundary was designed for the first one.

That's where things get interesting. 🚀

For space, here’s a Houston hotel option.

Yep. You mean traveling to another galaxy, not contemplating the void until it files a restraining order. 😄

The one you're thinking of is Andromeda Galaxy (M31).

5
🚀 The nearest major galaxy

Andromeda (M31) is about 2.5 million light-years away.

Us: Milky Way
Destination: Andromeda
Distance: ~2.5 million light-years
Diameter: ~220,000 light-years
Stars: roughly 1 trillion
We're already headed toward each other: Yep.
Collision: Expected in roughly 4–5 billion years

There are smaller satellite galaxies closer to us, such as the Magellanic Clouds, but if you're asking "what's the nearest proper big galaxy?" it's Andromeda.

And here's the absolutely deranged part

At 99.9% of light speed, ignoring the fact that getting a spaceship to that velocity is an engineering nightmare of biblical proportions:

From Earth's frame:

~2.5 million years pass.

For the travelers, special relativity changes the game. The trip's proper time could be dramatically shorter because of time dilation.

At 99.999999% of light speed, the Lorentz factor is about 7,071.

So:

2.5 million years / 7,071 ≈ 354 years

And at even more absurd velocities, you can theoretically reduce the travelers' experienced time to decades, years, or less.

The catch is that the energy required becomes monstrous, and accelerating/decelerating a substantial spacecraft to those velocities is currently nowhere remotely close to our capabilities.

But physics doesn't prohibit the journey merely because humans haven't figured out how to build the damn thing.

And if you're thinking beyond Andromeda, that's where intergalactic travel gets genuinely brutal. The Local Group is enormous, while the space between galaxy groups is really empty.

Yeah. Space is an absolute bastard. 😂

And your thought experiment actually lands on one of the central problems of interstellar civilization: the hard part isn't merely propulsion. It's preserving a functioning civilization during the journey.

Let's use your deliberately ridiculous-but-useful premise:

500 people per ship
~50 ships
25,000 initial settlers
Ships are effectively mobile towns
They're built in space because Earth's gravity well makes assembling gigantic structures increasingly stupid
Travel at 0.01c to 0.1c
Destination: Andromeda, because apparently the Milky Way wasn't sufficiently inconvenient
The travel time is the first slap in the face

Andromeda is ~2.5 million light-years away.

Cruise speed	Earth-frame travel time
1% c	250 million years
10% c	25 million years
50% c	5 million years
90% c	2.78 million years

So at 0.1c, you're not sending a generation ship.

You're sending a civilization fossil.

Even if you somehow solved propulsion, shielding, energy, and construction, your original 500 passengers aren't going to arrive.
